I don’t really see why everyone thinks I should put it back on.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I know other people say I’m too thin, but when I look in the mirror, especially before class or softball, my stomach and thighs still look bigger than they should. It makes me feel like I’m doing good or failing for the whole day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so. If I sit down for a real meal, it feels like it messes up what I’m trying to keep under control, so I end up just doing coffee before work, maybe a yogurt or a few bites later after class.\n\nDoctor: What is it that you're trying to keep under control when you think about eating?\n\nPatient: Mostly my body, I guess. Like if I eat fried food from the break room, or have a big plate at church, I start thinking it’s going to show on me right away. Then I keep thinking about it and I want to check the scale.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have lost weight. I don’t know what counts as “significant,” but my work pants are loose now, and people keep saying I look too thin. But when I see the number go down, it feels like I’m finally getting somewhere. I don’t really see why everyone thinks I should put it back on.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I know other people say I’m too thin, but when I look in the mirror, especially before class or softball, my stomach and thighs still look bigger than they should. It makes me feel like I’m doing good or failing for the whole day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so. I mean, sometimes I feel like I ate too much, but I wouldn’t say it’s a huge amount in an hour or two. I’m not sure what you’d count as large.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t make myself throw up after eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to try to burn off calories or control your weight?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. It can make me feel like I’m doing okay or like I’ve failed for the whole day.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your weight and body shape, do you think your perception of yourself is accurate?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s completely accurate. Like my weight and how I look. Before class or softball I’ll look in the mirror, and my stomach and thighs still look too big to me. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because the clinic kind of got on me about how little I’ve been eating. Most days I just have coffee before I go to the warehouse, and maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites after my GED class. Sitting down for a real meal just feels like it throws off what I’m trying to keep in line.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself not wanting to eat because you're just not hungry, or is it more that eating interferes with other things you'd rather be doing?\n\nPatient: It’s not really that I’m not hungry at all. If I sit down for a real meal, it feels like it messes up what I’m trying to keep under control, so I end up just doing coffee before work, maybe a yogurt or a few bites later after class.\n\nDoctor: What is it that you're trying to keep under control when you think about eating?\n\nPatient: Mostly my body, I guess. Like my weight and how I look. Before class or softball I’ll look in the mirror, and my stomach and thighs still look too big to me. Then it kind of sets the tone for the whole day, like I’m either doing good or I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I do get scared of gaining weight. Like if I eat fried food from the break room, or have a big plate at church, I start thinking it’s going to show on me right away. Then I keep thinking about it and I want to check the scale.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have lost weight. I don’t know what counts as “significant,” but my work pants are loose now, and people keep saying I look too thin. But when I see the number go down, it feels like I’m finally getting somewhere. I don’t really see why everyone thinks I should put it back on.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I know other people say I’m too thin, but when I look in the mirror, especially before class or softball, my stomach and thighs still look bigger than they should. It makes me feel like I’m doing good or failing for the whole day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so. I mean, sometimes I feel like I ate too much, but I wouldn’t say it’s a huge amount in an hour or two. I’m not sure what you’d count as large.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t make myself throw up after eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to try to burn off calories or control your weight?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do exercise sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s always to burn calories. I guess I pay attention to it, but… I’m not sure how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t use laxatives or water pills for that.\n\nDoctor: When you think your stomach and thighs look too big, do you think that's how other people see you as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I kind of do. Like if I look in the mirror before class or softball, my stomach and thighs just look too big to me, bigger than they should. And then I feel like other people can probably see that too, even if they don’t say anything. It can make me feel like I’m doing okay or like I’ve failed for the whole day.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your weight and body shape, do you think your perception of yourself is accurate?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s completely accurate. It feels accurate in the moment, like when I look in the mirror before class or softball and my stomach and thighs look bigger than they should. But I guess it also takes over too much, because then I feel like I’m doing good or like I failed for the whole day based on that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight and body shape are the most important things about you?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to say they’re the most important things, but… they feel really important. Like when I look in the mirror and my stomach or thighs look too big, it can decide how I feel about myself that whole day—like I’m doing okay or like I failed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that's much larger than what most people would eat in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because the clinic kind of got on me about how little I’ve been eating. Most days I just have coffee before I go to the warehouse, and maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites after my GED class. Sitting down for a real meal just feels like it throws off what I’m trying to keep in line.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself not wanting to eat because you're just not hungry, or is it more that eating interferes with other things you'd rather be doing?\n\nPatient: It’s not really that I’m not hungry at all. I mean, sometimes I can ignore it, but mostly eating feels like it gets in the way. If I sit down for a real meal, it feels like it messes up what I’m trying to keep under control, so I end up just doing coffee before work, maybe a yogurt or a few bites later after class.\n\nDoctor: What is it that you're trying to keep under control when you think about eating?\n\nPatient: Mostly my body, I guess. Like my weight and how I look. Before class or softball I’ll look in the mirror, and my stomach and thighs still look too big to me. Then it kind of sets the tone for the whole day, like I’m either doing good or I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I do get scared of gaining weight. Like if I eat fried food from the break room, or have a big plate at church, I start thinking it’s going to show on me right away. Then I keep thinking about it and I want to check the scale.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have lost weight. I don’t know what counts as “significant,” but my work pants are loose now, and people keep saying I look too thin. But when I see the number go down, it feels like I’m finally getting somewhere. I don’t really see why everyone thinks I should put it back on.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I know other people say I’m too thin, but when I look in the mirror, especially before class or softball, my stomach and thighs still look bigger than they should. It makes me feel like I’m doing good or failing for the whole day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so. I mean, sometimes I feel like I ate too much, but I wouldn’t say it’s a huge amount in an hour or two. I’m not sure what you’d count as large.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t make myself throw up after eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to try to burn off calories or control your weight?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do exercise sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s always to burn calories. I guess I pay attention to it, but… I’m not sure how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t use laxatives or water pills for that.\n\nDoctor: When you think your stomach and thighs look too big, do you think that's how other people see you as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I kind of do. Like if I look in the mirror before class or softball, my stomach and thighs just look too big to me, bigger than they should. And then I feel like other people can probably see that too, even if they don’t say anything. It can make me feel like I’m doing okay or like I’ve failed for the whole day.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your weight and body shape, do you think your perception of yourself is accurate?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s completely accurate. It feels accurate in the moment, like when I look in the mirror before class or softball and my stomach and thighs look bigger than they should. But I guess it also takes over too much, because then I feel like I’m doing good or like I failed for the whole day based on that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight and body shape are the most important things about you?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to say they’re the most important things, but… they feel really important. Like when I look in the mirror and my stomach or thighs look too big, it can decide how I feel about myself that whole day—like I’m doing okay or like I failed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that's much larger than what most people would eat in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think so… not like a huge amount in a short time. I haven’t really noticed that happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don’t think I’ve had times where I felt out of control eating. I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that's normal for you, but then still feel like you need to restrict your food intake afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that. Even if it’s not a lot, like just something normal for me, I’ll still feel like I need to cut back after. Most days I’m barely eating much anyway—coffee before the warehouse, maybe a yogurt or a few bites after GED class. Sitting down for a real meal just feels like it messes up what I’m trying to keep in line.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because the clinic kind of got on me about how little I’ve been eating. Most days I just have coffee before I go to the warehouse, and maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites after my GED class. Sitting down for a real meal just feels like it throws off what I’m trying to keep in line.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself not wanting to eat because you're just not hungry, or is it more that eating interferes with other things you'd rather be doing?\n\nPatient: It’s not really that I’m not hungry at all. I mean, sometimes I can ignore it, but mostly eating feels like it gets in the way. If I sit down for a real meal, it feels like it messes up what I’m trying to keep under control, so I end up just doing coffee before work, maybe a yogurt or a few bites later after class.\n\nDoctor: What is it that you're trying to keep under control when you think about eating?\n\nPatient: Mostly my body, I guess. Like my weight and how I look. Before class or softball I’ll look in the mirror, and my stomach and thighs still look too big to me. Then it kind of sets the tone for the whole day, like I’m either doing good or I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I do get scared of gaining weight. Like if I eat fried food from the break room, or have a big plate at church, I start thinking it’s going to show on me right away. Then I keep thinking about it and I want to check the scale.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have lost weight. I don’t know what counts as “significant,” but my work pants are loose now, and people keep saying I look too thin. But when I see the number go down, it feels like I’m finally getting somewhere. I don’t really see why everyone thinks I should put it back on.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I know other people say I’m too thin, but when I look in the mirror, especially before class or softball, my stomach and thighs still look bigger than they should. It makes me feel like I’m doing good or failing for the whole day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so. I mean, sometimes I feel like I ate too much, but I wouldn’t say it’s a huge amount in an hour or two. I’m not sure what you’d count as large.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t make myself throw up after eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to try to burn off calories or control your weight?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do exercise sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s always to burn calories. I guess I pay attention to it, but… I’m not sure how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t use laxatives or water pills for that.\n\nDoctor: When you think your stomach and thighs look too big, do you think that's how other people see you as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I kind of do. Like if I look in the mirror before class or softball, my stomach and thighs just look too big to me, bigger than they should. And then I feel like other people can probably see that too, even if they don’t say anything. It can make me feel like I’m doing okay or like I’ve failed for the whole day.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your weight and body shape, do you think your perception of yourself is accurate?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s completely accurate. It feels accurate in the moment, like when I look in the mirror before class or softball and my stomach and thighs look bigger than they should. But I guess it also takes over too much, because then I feel like I’m doing good or like I failed for the whole day based on that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight and body shape are the most important things about you?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to say they’re the most important things, but… they feel really important. Like when I look in the mirror and my stomach or thighs look too big, it can decide how I feel about myself that whole day—like I’m doing okay or like I failed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that's much larger than what most people would eat in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think so… not like a huge amount in a short time. I haven’t really noticed that happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don’t think I’ve had times where I felt out of control eating. I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that's normal for you, but then still feel like you need to restrict your food intake afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that. Even if it’s not a lot, like just something normal for me, I’ll still feel like I need to cut back after. Most days I’m barely eating much anyway—coffee before the warehouse, maybe a yogurt or a few bites after GED class. Sitting down for a real meal just feels like it messes up what I’m trying to keep in line.\n\nDoctor: When you think about gaining weight, do you imagine that it would change your body shape in a way that would be unacceptable to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Like if I eat fried food from the break room, or if I have a big plate at church, I start thinking it’s going to show on my body right away. It feels unacceptable, like I need to know if it changed something, so I’ll want to check the scale.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Sitting down for a real meal just feels like it throws off what I’m trying to keep in line.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself not wanting to eat because you're just not hungry, or is it more that eating interferes with other things you'd rather be doing?\n\nPatient: It’s not really that I’m not hungry at all. I mean, sometimes I can ignore it, but mostly eating feels like it gets in the way. If I sit down for a real meal, it feels like it messes up what I’m trying to keep under control, so I end up just doing coffee before work, maybe a yogurt or a few bites later after class.\n\nDoctor: What is it that you're trying to keep under control when you think about eating?\n\nPatient: Mostly my body, I guess. Like my weight and how I look. Before class or softball I’ll look in the mirror, and my stomach and thighs still look too big to me. Then it kind of sets the tone for the whole day, like I’m either doing good or I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I do get scared of gaining weight. Like if I eat fried food from the break room, or have a big plate at church, I start thinking it’s going to show on me right away. Then I keep thinking about it and I want to check the scale.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have lost weight. I don’t know what counts as “significant,” but my work pants are loose now, and people keep saying I look too thin. But when I see the number go down, it feels like I’m finally getting somewhere. I don’t really see why everyone thinks I should put it back on.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I know other people say I’m too thin, but when I look in the mirror, especially before class or softball, my stomach and thighs still look bigger than they should. It makes me feel like I’m doing good or failing for the whole day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so. I mean, sometimes I feel like I ate too much, but I wouldn’t say it’s a huge amount in an hour or two. I’m not sure what you’d count as large.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t make myself throw up after eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to try to burn off calories or control your weight?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do exercise sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s always to burn calories. I guess I pay attention to it, but… I’m not sure how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t use laxatives or water pills for that.\n\nDoctor: When you think your stomach and thighs look too big, do you think that's how other people see you as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I kind of do. Like if I look in the mirror before class or softball, my stomach and thighs just look too big to me, bigger than they should. And then I feel like other people can probably see that too, even if they don’t say anything. It can make me feel like I’m doing okay or like I’ve failed for the whole day.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your weight and body shape, do you think your perception of yourself is accurate?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s completely accurate. It feels accurate in the moment, like when I look in the mirror before class or softball and my stomach and thighs look bigger than they should. But I guess it also takes over too much, because then I feel like I’m doing good or like I failed for the whole day based on that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight and body shape are the most important things about you?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to say they’re the most important things, but… they feel really important. Like when I look in the mirror and my stomach or thighs look too big, it can decide how I feel about myself that whole day—like I’m doing okay or like I failed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that's much larger than what most people would eat in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think so… not like a huge amount in a short time. I haven’t really noticed that happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don’t think I’ve had times where I felt out of control eating. I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that's normal for you, but then still feel like you need to restrict your food intake afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that. Even if it’s not a lot, like just something normal for me, I’ll still feel like I need to cut back after. Most days I’m barely eating much anyway—coffee before the warehouse, maybe a yogurt or a few bites after GED class. Sitting down for a real meal just feels like it messes up what I’m trying to keep in line.\n\nDoctor: When you think about gaining weight, do you imagine that it would change your body shape in a way that would be unacceptable to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Like if I eat fried food from the break room, or if I have a big plate at church, I start thinking it’s going to show on my body right away. It feels unacceptable, like I need to know if it changed something, so I’ll want to check the scale.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten a normal amount of food, but then still feel the need to restrict or make up for it afterwards, while also having times where you eat much more than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I feel like I have to restrict after eating what’s probably a normal amount. But I don’t really have times where I eat way more than usual. Most days it’s more like I’m barely eating—coffee before the warehouse, maybe a yogurt or a few bites after GED class. Sitting down for a real meal just feels like it throws everything off.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have times where you eat much more than you normally would in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don’t think I have times where I eat a lot in a short period. It’s more that I worry about even normal amounts, but I wouldn’t say I binge or anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’m not sure what you’d count as large.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t make myself throw up after eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to try to burn off calories or control your weight?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do exercise sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s always to burn calories. I guess I pay attention to it, but… I’m not sure how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t use laxatives or water pills for that.\n\nDoctor: When you think your stomach and thighs look too big, do you think that's how other people see you as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I kind of do. Like if I look in the mirror before class or softball, my stomach and thighs just look too big to me, bigger than they should. And then I feel like other people can probably see that too, even if they don’t say anything. It can make me feel like I’m doing okay or like I’ve failed for the whole day.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your weight and body shape, do you think your perception of yourself is accurate?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s completely accurate. It feels accurate in the moment, like when I look in the mirror before class or softball and my stomach and thighs look bigger than they should. But I guess it also takes over too much, because then I feel like I’m doing good or like I failed for the whole day based on that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight and body shape are the most important things about you?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to say they’re the most important things, but… they feel really important. Like when I look in the mirror and my stomach or thighs look too big, it can decide how I feel about myself that whole day—like I’m doing okay or like I failed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that's much larger than what most people would eat in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think so… not like a huge amount in a short time. I haven’t really noticed that happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don’t think I’ve had times where I felt out of control eating. I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that's normal for you, but then still feel like you need to restrict your food intake afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that. Even if it’s not a lot, like just something normal for me, I’ll still feel like I need to cut back after. Most days I’m barely eating much anyway—coffee before the warehouse, maybe a yogurt or a few bites after GED class. Sitting down for a real meal just feels like it messes up what I’m trying to keep in line.\n\nDoctor: When you think about gaining weight, do you imagine that it would change your body shape in a way that would be unacceptable to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Like if I eat fried food from the break room, or if I have a big plate at church, I start thinking it’s going to show on my body right away. It feels unacceptable, like I need to know if it changed something, so I’ll want to check the scale.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten a normal amount of food, but then still feel the need to restrict or make up for it afterwards, while also having times where you eat much more than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I feel like I have to restrict after eating what’s probably a normal amount. But I don’t really have times where I eat way more than usual. Most days it’s more like I’m barely eating—coffee before the warehouse, maybe a yogurt or a few bites after GED class. Sitting down for a real meal just feels like it throws everything off.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have times where you eat much more than you normally would in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don’t think I have times where I eat a lot in a short period. It’s more that I worry about even normal amounts, but I wouldn’t say I binge or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel like you've lost control and eaten a lot more than you normally would?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I can’t really remember a time where I felt out of control and ate a lot more than I usually would. It’s more that I get uncomfortable with eating, even if it’s not that much."
